In the directional parts there are red lotuses, and by the power of the Victor, white lotuses also in the corners,
distinguished by moon and sun; and likewise those of the immortals, with twice fourteen petals.
The five lines, sky and the rest, by the power of the qualities of their nature, stand at intervals of a third.

At the door corners below the pillars are the wind and other maṇḍalas of the nāgas.
In the northeastern and southwestern corners, set on the earth maṇḍala, are the moon and the sun, O lord of men.
On the upper part of the outer doors is a wheel of Dharma with deer, the color of a cloud.
On the right is a red vase, together with Kubera; at the water, a great drum and the bodhi tree.

Bells, mirrors and banners, white as the moon, hang down from the archways,
and the half-necklaces are white in color; by the power of the family, the chariots are in the middle of the doors.
White and red wheels, by the power of their own Victor's family, are on the wind outside the doors,
and with blazing, radiating vajras, O lord of men, are the outer vajra garlands too.

Thick lines bring diseases, and thin and faulty lines plainly bring loss of wealth;
broken lines bring death, and crooked ones, O lord of men, shake the town together with its king.
If the emblems, sun and moon, are broken, mantra practitioners gain no accomplishment that overcomes the terrors of existence.
Mixing them cuts off the family line; the colored powder maṇḍalas should be understood according to the families of the Victors.

Any emblem that the Victor did not clearly teach in the tantra, used for the sake of beauty,
should not be placed in the maṇḍalas: it is a sign of Māra for the family that gives birth to the Victors.
Therefore the emblems are those taught in the tantra, by the power of the family, from the door corners to the maṇḍala.
At the end of the half-necklaces, on the base of the earth maṇḍala, lotuses, vases and the like should be made beautifully.

Black and the rest, increasing by quarters, are the heights of the colored powder, rising by a single barleycorn each.
The walls are threefold, and the lines of sun, moon and lotus are twofold.
From the cores outward the height becomes double, O lord of men, in this maṇḍala,
and at the end of the half-necklaces it is one barleycorn, for the earth, water, fire and wind and the vajra garlands.

From the center to the first corner of the doors is the earth, for the maṇḍala of the world-system.
From the door to the edge of the carcikā, thrice eight squares make the place of the salt ocean.
Then, with twenty-four squares, the fire maṇḍala, and after that, likewise, the wind.
Outside of that, through their purification, earth, water, fire and wind are to be seen.

Crown protuberance, face and neck: with thrice eight squares the maṇḍala is to be purified.
Then all of Meru, with thrice eight squares, as far as the earth extends;
then with six squares each, in order, clearly, the realm of the nāgas and the seven underworlds.
In this way all of these, earth and the rest, are again likewise to be purified in one's own body.

Two, four and one, four and one, twelve and three, six, seven and two, half of three and half of three,
three and three, divided, and three and six as well: these are the parts, in order, in units of three,
for the core, the hub, the spokes or the lotus petals, and then the pillars and the vajra garland,
the lotus and vajra garlands, the grounds, and then the doors, the door surrounds and so forth,

the pillars, the walls and the parapets, and again then the frieze and the grounds of the necklaces,
and the mirror ground becomes the frieze, O lord of men, by the parts declared for the archways.
Outside, all the doors and the rest become double, and further outside, double that again.
Outside, the lotus wheels and the chariot maṇḍalas are made with twelve.

On the lotus of the supreme Victor, born from the gnosis of the syllable oṃ, upon seats of moon and sun,
one should place a and the rest and kā and the rest, together with the empty, and the Mother who gives birth to the three realms of existence.
From the supreme, the empty, the syllable a, the visarga, and the vowelless, the vajras of body, speech and mind
come forth; and the gnosis vajra, the bliss-maker of the supreme source of mantras, is the fourth.

On the many-colored lotus of the Lord of Victors, upon moon, sun and fire, is the syllable hūṃ;
on the petals of the cardinal directions, a and the rest with the empty, and on the intermediate petals ha and the rest with the empty, four kinds.
In the corners of Īśāna, Nairṛti, Agni the Crested One, and Vāyu are body, speech, mind and passion;
the syllables hī and the rest become the ten of the vases, ending here with those called haṃ haḥ.

On the eastern lotus is the syllable i; in Agni's quarter, abiding on a lotus, is the long syllable ī.
In the quarters of Yama and the asura are the syllables ṛ; in those of Kubera and Hara abide the short and long syllables u.
In the corners of the waters and of Vāyu, abiding on lotuses, are the short and long syllables ḷ;
they are to be set out according to the distinctions of the faces: black, red, white, and like the finest gold.

South of the eastern gate and on Agni's lotus abide the short and long likewise;
likewise the pair of syllables ar abides in the quarters of Yama and the asura, and in the west the pair of syllables al.
o au are in the quarters of the yakṣa and Rudra; and north of the gates of the god, of the waters, of Yama and of the yakṣa
are aṃ aḥ and the rest, in order; and again ya ra va la on the lotuses of the gates of the god and the rest.

At the eastern and northern gates, the hare-bearing moons become the seats of the wrathful ones;
in the south and the west, suns on lotuses certainly become the seats of the two.
Through the distinctions of wisdom and means, moons and suns on lotuses also become seats:
to the right and at the back they become suns, and in the quarters of the lord of the gods and of Kubera, moon seats.

The syllables of the classes beginning with ka, distinguished by the forms of the bindu and abiding on lotuses,
are arranged in order on the root, the stem, the petals, the filaments and the pericarps:
from earth up to space, ka kha ga gha ṅa, short and long, each on its own ground.
The bindu becomes the moon and the visarga the sun, and the seats are upon the pericarps.

The six classes, short and long, by virtue of their nature and qualities, on the plinths beside the pillars,
are the goddesses of scent and the rest, abiding in order on the ground of their own family, from the eastern side in their own directions.
Outside, distinguished by the bindu and the rest, three times twelve, on the plinths likewise,
all the desire goddesses abide everywhere in the directions of their own families, according to the distinctions of the colors of the Victors.

The short and long syllables ha, in the quarters of the god, of Kubera, the west and the south, are those of Carcikā and the rest;
the syllables kṣa of Vaiṣṇavī and the rest, distinguished by a and the rest, are in the quarters of Agni, Hara, Vāyu and the asura.
hi and the rest and kṣi and the rest, eight in number, abide on the lotus petals, front and back, in their own directions.
ya and the rest, the six short and long, are on the lotus petals of the god and the lotuses of Agni, in order.

Likewise on the lotus petals of Yama and the asura, distinguished as short and long, are ra and the rest;
in the quarters of the yakṣa and Rudra, va and the rest; and on the lotus petals of Vāyu, together with the Lord of the Waters, la and the rest.
South of the eastern gate also, abiding on lotus petals and distinguished by the vowels, is the class of ca;
the short belong to the asura, and the long, abiding in Agni's corner, become those of Vāyu.

Likewise in Yama's quarter, the class of ṭa belonging to Agni and to the corpse-faced one, distinguished as short and long;
on the left and in Īśāna's quarter it becomes the class of pa, and with the Lord of the Waters, distinguished as long, it belongs to Gaṇapati.
Belonging to Śakra and to Brahmā, in the waters and in the wind, short and long, is the class of ta.
North of the eastern gate, on the lotus petals, the class of ka belonging to the Enemy of the Asuras becomes short,

and in the south it becomes long, belonging to the Yamas, O lord of men; and in the north and the west as well,
short and long, belonging to Paśupati and Jambhala, it becomes the class of sa, O king.
They become the own seeds of the asura and the rest; on the petals, for the asura, the end and the middle of each one's own class;
and on the twenty-eight petals as well, by virtue of the days, the syllables of each one's own class.

The six, yā rā vā lā haṃ haḥdrug, are certainly on the chariots, above and at the root, in the quarter of the god and the rest.
To the right and left of the gates, those of the nāgas, abiding in ya and the rest, become the syllables ha.
The forms in which the six classes are stacked, and again ya and the rest joined with ha yar va la and with kṣa and the rest,
are in the wheel of the directions: the classes of ka and the rest abide in the maṇḍala of wind, and ca and the rest in the others, in corresponding order,

in the east, Yama's quarter, the north, the waters, Agni's quarter, the asura's, and the corners of Īśāna and Vāyu.
aṃ aḥ, of moon and sun, abiding on the ground of the charnel grounds, are the mantras of the hosts of bhūtas.
The syllable hūṃ becomes the wheel of Dharma, and likewise the syllable āḥ becomes the seeds of the vases.
The syllable oṃ becomes that of the great drum, and in the water ho becomes that of the bodhi trees.

Thus those endowed with vowels, by virtue of family, become the distinct mantras in the maṇḍalas.
All the colors of the mudrās and emblems come about through the distinctions of the faces of the Vajra Holder.
In the hearth the fire offerings are likewise, and again the invitation of those at the fords,
the purification of the glorious ground, the rite of the guest water, and likewise the vanquishing of the māras.

To guard the gates, pure disciples who keep the vows faithfully should be stationed.
The yoginīs of the glorious vases are in order in the corners of Agni, the asura, Vāyu and Īśāna.
The master becomes the glorious lord of the assembly; having appointed, O lord of men, the holders of the action vajra,
if there are no disciples, the lord of the assembly should himself perform the fire offerings and the other rites.

Round, or with corners numbering the Vedas, according to family, are the hearths for pacifying and increasing;
northern, or with the corners of the fierce rites, and also on white ground, the root lotuses are doubled.
The depth is the measure of the lotus, and within it is the root lotus together with its emblem.

At half a lotus, outside the lotus, it bears the emblems, sword, jewel and the rest, together with vases.
By half of that is the lip, and twice that again is the portion of the rims;
above, vajra garlands too are surely raised to a height of half the lip.
Outside and below, lotus petals and an arrangement of kuśa grass should be laid in all directions;
at its edge, in the west, there is surely a single door of three lines.

The masters' seats are surely level, twice the size of the central lotus.
To the left is the seat for the water offering (arghya), and to the right, O lord of men, are the vessels of the ladles for the fire offering.
All bear the emblem of the vajra of the Lord of Victors, or else the sky-lotus of the Mother.
Mouth and secret place, too, become two kinds of hearth, O king: the outer one and the one in the body.

Having made protection over the ten-direction maṇḍala of the hearth with the wrathful kings together with their goddesses,
having purified and so forth with the glorious vajras, the water offering is made to the fire itself with water and flowers.
One endowed with its yoga should offer it, having meditated on a moon in the lotus of one's own heart.
With one face, white in color, his four lotus hands holding in the left a water pot and a lotus,

in the right a staff and a rosary, his matted locks very tawny, with reddish-yellow eyes, and clothed in garments:
at the fire's heart, upon a moon, one should surely meditate on a hook arising from a stainless, radiating jaḥ.
Make the all-pervading wisdom being drawn in by it perfectly one in taste with its own body.
Thus the hearth too is made perfect, O lord of men, and likewise the fire is invited.

With the thumbs of the boon-granting hands one should perform the fire offering into the mouth of the fire.
With the limb of the chief vajra, too, with a hundred kindling sticks, grain, dūrva grass, butter and milk,
seated cross-legged, deeply peaceful, with an unwavering mind, the knower of secret mantra, whose body is mantra,
the master who holds the action vajra, should then surely give the burnt offerings to the fire.

Having performed the fire offering in sequence, likewise give sprinkling water and water to drink, both to the fire and to oneself;
having offered scents and the rest and incense, one should request the wisdom fire to depart into the lotus of one's own heart.
Afterwards the glorious guru, treasury of all qualities, surely bestows empowerment on the disciple.
First come the five nectars, blessed by the vajra of the supreme Victor, for the sake of purification.

When the fire offering has been accomplished with one's own mantra, the colored powders laid down and the mantra emblems arranged,
white crystal vases wound with five threads should be properly set at the corners.
The master, facing east, having invited the wisdom wheel with the swift-moving wrathful kings born of vajra and lotus,
should make it one in taste with the colored-powder maṇḍala, for the sake of empowerment.

For all the gods and goddesses, it is to be spoken before their names.
For the fire offering, the heart mantra ends in svāhā; likewise for offerings to them it ends in namaḥ.
jaḥ hūṃ baṃ hoḥ, set out in order with the hook and the rest, serve for inviting, causing to enter, binding,
and pleasing; and for giving the water offering it becomes idaṃ gṛhṇa gṛhṇa arghakaṃ me.

For increase the mantra ends in svāhā, O lord of men; for pacification here it ends in namaḥ.
For summoning it ends in gauṣaṭ, and likewise for subjugation it ends in bauṣaṭ.
Ending in the syllable hūṃ is for fierce rites; and by the power of the qualities of nature, ending in phaṭ is for striking with the ritual dagger and the like.
White, red, black and like the finest gold: such are the mantras, by the distinctions of the rites.

In the east the glorious mind vajra, like a dark cloud; in the north the body vajra;
in the south the speech vajra; and in the west the wisdom vajra, abiding in the direction of its own family.
White, red and yellow, by the power of their families, pervade the portions of the ground.
The birthplaces of the gods of wind, fire, water and earth (Pṛthivī), O lord of men, are i ṛ u ḷ.

oṃ āḥ hūṃ are the three seals; together with the word svāhā and through the distinction of the long vowels they are also five.
hūṃ āḥ oṃ hoḥraṅ are their own faces, and likewise the other becomes the fifth, the indestructible.
The six syllables hraṃ together with a and the rest, with the six endings namaḥ and so on, become the six glorious limbs.
The syllable phreṃ is that of Viśvamātā, and the tenfold stacked mantra is that of the Victors.

The syllable hūṃ, the seed of wisdom, is the heart; and the great stacked mantra is joined with the faces.
The six limbs beginning with the syllable hūṃ, having the body vajra first: this is the near-heart.
The garland mantras of the gods and goddesses are likewise other; through the many distinctions of rites
they should be known to become manifold here in the Kālacakra maṇḍala, by the power of the qualities of nature.

With jewels, gold, camphor and flowers, garments of many kinds, scents, incense and lamps,
bells, mirrors and canopies, many kinds of fruit, banners and so forth, and with dance and music,
having made a varied offering, and the other tenfold offerings too, according to one's own capacity, as has been taught,
at the root of the masters' feet the supreme son should present the fee for the sake of purification.

Substances of the three purities, and oneself, together with sons and daughters, and a young woman born of noble family:
"From this very day, I, fearful of terrible saṃsāra, go especially for refuge to the Victors,
to the lotuses of your feet, which surely steal away the fear of existence, with pure body, speech and mind."
With these words, having made a maṇḍala offering with flowers and gold, one supplicates the guru.

"For the vajra of desire, I shall take upon the crown of my head the vajra, bell, mudrā and the guru.
For the jewel, I shall give gifts; for this wheel, I shall keep the pledges of the supreme Victor.
For the sword, I shall make offerings; and clearly, for the lotus family, I shall keep the vows.
For the liberation of beings, for the family that gives rise to the Victors, I shall generate awakening."

The disciple, having bathed, anointed with scent, and bound by definite observances, is placed on the eastern ground,
and for the sake of accomplishment is given a tooth stick consecrated by the vajras of the supreme Victor.
On the tongue too, nectar is surely placed by the pledges of the supreme Victor, for the sake of bringing down the fragrance.

The mantra is the single syllable hūṃ, together with a ra la: this urges on the chief of the wrathful ones.

When the king of the wrathful ones descends, with hands beautiful with weapons one threatens the hosts of Māra,
and with stances such as the left-extended, and with gestures of many kinds, one dances the vajra dance.
With laughter mixed with the syllable hūṃ one strikes fear into enemies, and one also sings the vajra song.
One becomes shameless and fearless; and other deities, by the power of their qualities, are peaceful.

When body descends, the yogin performs the actions of body, by the power of its nature and qualities.
When speech descends, he becomes a speaker victorious over gods, bhūtas and asuras.
When mind descends, he comes to know what dwells in the hearts of others, and all that has been and will be.
When gnosis descends, he becomes the sole Teacher, endowed with miraculous powers, the guru even of the gurus of the buddhas.

When earth descends, the yogins become like mountains; and with water they become cool.
When fire descends, they burn; and with wind, likewise, they become dry.
When space descends, they become invisible; upon the earth they become sky-goers.
Likewise all, form and the rest, should be understood in their order, by the power of their nature and qualities.

The descent will certainly take place, O lord of men, through the power of the mantrins' meditation,
through the divisions of approach, and through recitation of the various pledge mantras and so on, at certain times;
and in certain maṇḍalas, for the supreme son, through tasting the buddhas, by the power of the nectars.
But when lowly beings of the many kinds of existence take possession by their own blessing, there is no accomplishment for mantrins.

Having dismissed the descent, afterward one should protect the disciple with the Victors at the head, the heart, the crown, the navel, the throat
and the secret place, and with one's own three vajras, abiding in the place of one's own family.
Having given him yellow garments for his body, the disciple, his eyes covered, should be led in here.
For the sake of the conventional, to those who take up the most supreme observances, those very observances should be given:

Harming, untruth, the wives of others, which are to be abandoned, the wealth of others, and likewise drinking liquor:
these five sins are vajra nooses in saṃsāra that destroy one's own virtue.
They should be conferred in the name of whoever, at whatever time, has appeared as the guru of gods and humans.
This command of the Universal Lord destroys the fears of existence, and you too must keep it.

Gambling, blameworthy food, reciting evil words, the religion of the bhūtas, and the religion of the lord of the asuras;
killing cattle, children, women and men, and the guru of gods and humans: these five one must not do.
Resentment toward friends, toward masters, toward the gurus of gods and humans, toward the Saṅgha, and toward those who trust one;
and attachment of the senses: thus are the twenty-five observances of the lords of existence.

Consecrated with the glorious mantra, a single flower should be given into the joined lotus hands.
First, having circled three times, the disciple casts the flower from his joined lotus hands into the maṇḍala.
Wherever the good flower falls, O lord of men, that is certainly his family.
Afterward the seven empowerments, and here the unsurpassed threefold one, should be properly conferred as is fitting.

With golden vases set with jewels, eight and four, or, O king, with vases of earthen nature set with jewels,
with the victorious and the all-victorious vases holding medicines and scents, the goddesses perform the bathing.
Through the division of the buddhas the crown is given; and by the supreme guru, through the śaktis, the silk ribbon.
Vajra and bell come from sun and moon; and the observances too should be joined with the objects together with the senses.

Through the wrathful ones the names, Maitrī and the rest, are clearly given; and by the Lord of Victors the permission together with its bestowal.
Having given vajra and bell, the pure Dharma should be taught with supreme compassion:
For the vajra family, certainly, one should take life; for the sword, untruthful speech;
for the jewel, one should steal the wealth of others; and for the supreme lotus family, steal the wives of others.

Liquor, lamps, buddhas and all excellent objects should be relied on by the wheel.
For the curved knife, the lotus of space of all women, caṇḍālīs and the rest, should not be disparaged.
For the sake of beings this body, together with its wealth, should be given away; you should not guard it.
O child of good family, the Victor has said that buddhahood comes about in this way, and not otherwise through endless eons.

The water is Tārā and the other goddesses; what is called the crown is the Victors; the silk ribbon of the heroes is the śaktis;
the vajra and bell are sun and moon; the observances are the objects; and the name is the joining of Maitrī and the rest.
The permission is perfect awakening, the Glorious Lady, and Kālacakra, destroyer of the fears of existence; so it should be understood.
These seven empowerments, which remove the stain of sin, should be properly conferred in the maṇḍala.

Empowered by the seven empowerments, by the power of virtue one becomes lord of the seven grounds.
Empowered by the vase and the secret, one certainly enters further into the irreversible and the rest.
Empowered by the wisdom-gnosis, one becomes Mañjughoṣa, destroyer of the fears of existence.
If, through the power of deceit, a root downfall occurs, one goes to hell; these are suffering.

Root downfalls are purified for those endowed with qualities who abide in the seven empowerments.
But for the vase and the secret, owing to the binding force of the observances, there is no purification at the higher level.
If a root downfall occurs, whoever it is must enter this maṇḍala again in order to be purified;
and having obtained permission once more, in the gathered assembly the name of the eldest becomes that of the youngest.

From disturbing the mind of the glorious guru comes the disciples' root downfall that is the hare-bearer, the first.
Through disregarding his command comes the "other," the second; and likewise, certainly, anger toward vajra siblings is the third.
Abandoning love becomes the fourth; and the degeneration of Bodhicitta becomes the arrow, the fifth.
The sixth is disparaging the tenets; and the mountains, the seventh, is giving the secret to those who are not ripened.

Tormenting the aggregates becomes the nāga, the eighth; and again the ninth is lack of faith in the pure Dharma.
Deceitful love, and conceptualizing that which is free of names and so forth and bestows bliss, are the directions and Raudra, the tenth and eleventh.

Reviling pure sentient beings is the sun, the twelfth; abandoning the pledges one has received is yet another, the thirteenth;
and reviling all women is surely Manu, the fourteenth. These are the downfalls for those who abide in the Vajra Vehicle.

With eight and four vases of jewels and gold, or, O king, vases of earthen make containing jewels,
and with the Victory and Complete Victory vases filled with medicines and scents, one bathes the disciple in the center of the seat.
The nāgas bind on the glorious crown, and on the eight-petaled lotus the four bind on the silk ribbon.
For the consort it is done with the glorious vase; here, without the lotus, the five lines are left out.

At the first, with the waters, one becomes a layman; with the vase as well, one becomes a novice;
at the secret empowerment a monk, and at the higher act one comes to be called an elder.
The mudrā, the silk ribbon, the crowns, the vajra, and what is held for worship are to be given by the supreme guru.
For one who is to be a master, these are given according to his own Jina family; the mudrā is completely pure.

Above, a canopy is set up, and on the surface of the ground three lines are drawn all around.
Beneath them are vases of jewels and gold filled with water, wound with thread, with lotuses at their mouths.
In conches and the like, or in vessels of gold or of silver, scented water is to be prepared.
In the center a gleaming seat of gold is set out, and on it the bathing is to be begun.

With flowers and the like, and with scented sesame oil ripened by the sun's fire, the deity is to be anointed.
Having rubbed it down with powders, it is to be washed with honey, butter, curds, milk and water.
Having waved over it white mustard seeds, lamps and many kinds of excellent fruit,
it is to be moved from its place, and its body is wholly covered with red cloths.

At the edge of the glorious maṇḍala, on a level piece of ground, five lines are made by the Jina measure,
and in the center an eight-petaled lotus adorned with five emblems set in the directions of their own families.
In its center is to be placed the god or goddess with the lotus of the face turned toward the west,
or stūpas and the like, or books, or likewise what is called a painted scroll, with the mantra practitioner facing it.

Having made the supreme Jinas, together with the vajras of body, speech and mind, into the nature of emptiness,
one then meditates, by the methods described before, on Kālacakra born from moon, sun and vajra.
The knowledge goddesses and the rest, and the buddhas born of the families of the vowels and consonants, generated each from its own seed,
are to be meditated upon in the cakras of the heart, throat, navel, secret place, head and crown, according to family.

Thus one should meditate with certainty, and then once more, upon the bodies of all the deities,
the kings of the wrathful ones summon and cause to enter the wisdom beings, without existence yet equal to existence;
and the binding, the pleasing and the making of one taste are likewise done upon the deities' own bodies.
Then the master himself should bow down to the deity whose face has been made fully clear.

The seed that abides in the qualities of the gods' and goddesses' own families, drawn from ā and the rest and kā and the rest,
that very seed of theirs, at the center of the heart, resting on the union of moon and sun, endowed with body, speech and mind;
the limbs of the thirty-two major marks abiding throughout the whole body, and the minor marks, space and eight, the eighty,
divided out by the letters: these very things one should meditate upon in the books, clearly, leaf by leaf.

In the heart of the stūpa the glorious wheel, vajra, jewel and lotus, and in the north the sword.
On the rosary the syllable hūṃ; the beads that are counted are the major and minor marks.
On the body of the bell the vowels, ten multiplied by three, and on the vajra the ka and other classes.
On what is held for worship those very same; and on the upper garment the minor marks, eight multiplied by ten.

The ka and other classes together with the vowels, space, taste and qualities, are to be meditated upon in the meditation belts.
haṃ haḥ on the glorious earrings, and the pair called aṃ aḥ on the necklace and the girdle.
Likewise the pair of syllables a goes to the bracelets, and ha hā to the anklets.
The five aspects of emptiness that abide throughout the whole body are to be meditated upon in the ashes.

From the wisdom aspects of one's own body, all the aggregates, elements and the rest, together with the three vajras,
are to be set in the heart lotuses of the deities, in the order of each one's own seed.
When the seeds are set, consecration takes place, O lord of men, in stūpas, images and the like.
At the time of withdrawal, with the vajra of the hand, one should draw the seeds into one's own body.

Here, and also in the first case, for painted scrolls the bathing is done upon a mirror.
Afterward, with scents, incense, most fragrant flowers, song and music,
dance, many kinds of excellent garments, yak-tail fans and parasols, one should worship the deity.
Having thus performed the consecration, one gives the Saṅgha a feast of many excellent flavors.

In wells, ponds and lakes, in the directions and intermediate directions, the eight nāga kings are to be set.
The seven oceans are put in with their own seeds, and the five scents are to be added with honey and water.
At the end of the fire offering, in the ponds and the like, one should meditate on Varuṇa, white, a noose in his hand.
In a park, one tree is made a wish-fulfilling tree, after empowering it to abide in all the trees.

The crown, the silk ribbons, the necklace and bracelets, and likewise the earrings, the girdle and so forth,
are to be given to the master, O lord of men, and also a fee, as far as one is able.
The patron, for the sake of merit, should strive for the good of others toward the whole assembly:
"By this merit, may the beings who dwell in threefold existence attain unsurpassed awakening!"

As long as the years are those of the directions, the single one becomes tenfold: one should look and touch.
Then one should embrace, and resort to the moving ocean together with taste and the rest.
From twenty years upward the supreme mudrā is the Terrifying One, the portion of the wrathful ones, the bhūtas and the asuras.
For empowerment, six and four, giving the fruit of equal bliss; the others are for meditation.

The first touching of the breasts of the glorious Prajñā is itself the vase empowerment.

From the secret places, by tasting and by seeing the hare-bearer (the moon), it becomes the secret empowerment.
For the wisdom-gnosis empowerment, once all her limbs and her face have been purified with the families of the Victors,
here the guru, having made the Victors his witnesses,
should give the mudrā to the disciple.

She is adorned with every ornament, like refined gold, a lovely girl twelve years of age;
with her, who has her blood, the two who embody wisdom and means enjoy desire with his own vajra-jewel.
Then, knowing the disciple to be pure, he casts the vajra together with its seed into his mouth,
and afterward gives him his own mudrā. Then, once more, the other empowerment is the one endowed with the path of smoke and the rest.

One who is fearful, whose mind is deluded, deceitful, under another's control, who roams about, who is diseased, who has given birth,
angry, proud, craving wealth, untruthful, fond of quarrels, defective in her own limbs, or impure:
these prajñās are to be shunned in the empowerment by gurus who are highly skilled, O lord of men.
Those described before, devoted to the Buddha and keeping the guru's samaya, are to be bowed to and honored.

Desire is what stirs; it moves in one's own mind. The fillings become filling itself.
From the supreme limbs, the hare-bearer is made into liquid, and the excellent drop flows.
Drawn up by the oḍḍa, it gives supreme bliss by its very nature; the drops are released at the end of the three.
These blisses of flowing, in seeing, touching and union, are distinguished by the divisions of joy and so forth.

Desire brings about joy, and the first is in men's looking with the eyes.
Afterward, in the full joining of filling, there is once more supreme joy, in one's own body.
Blazing, the flowing of the drop, and joy again: this is special joy, by the vajra in the lotus.
Oḍḍa: abiding in the drop's coming to the end of the three, the vajra brings about innate joy.

Desire, joy, trembling and changelessness: by these four that union is the first.
Filling, potency, birth and supreme joy are certainly the second.
Blazing, the drop, swooning, and again special joy are the third.
Oḍḍa, nāda, sleep and innate joy become the fourth.

By the mind's intention she becomes the mother; by touching and seeing, the sister.
When the vajra has entered, with the act, the daughter; with great joy, the niece likewise.
When the drop falls, the wife; and from the waning of passion, the yoginī who dwells in another family.
These are the six mudrās of yoga: earth, water, fire the eater of offerings, wind, space, and the entities of annihilation.

The vajra fully enters the lotus, the wind enters the crested fire, and the drop does not fall: this is the third.
These, made fully clear by the three yogas, are definitively the mudrās of body, speech and mind.
The ultimate of passion and dispassion, the primordial treasure of supreme bliss to be realized by yogins, is the fourth.
Among the mudrās, she becomes the excellent mother in ten ways: this is from the mouth of the glorious guru.

The Prajñā mother is the supreme mother, who gives birth to the three worlds; Locanā and the rest are the sisters.
The six vajra goddesses are the nieces; Carcikā and the others, who terrify the beastlike folk, are the grandnieces.
At all times while abiding in the wheel, those who dwell in the ground of one's own family are to be relied upon by the yogin.
In fields, sacred places and charnel grounds, whether peopled or unpeopled, they should never be forsaken.

The glorious vajra-holder is the glorious father, who generates the three worlds; all the buddhas are the brothers.
Locanā and the rest are the brothers' children; the others, of many kinds, are the grandchildren and the grandchildren's children.
Abiding in the wheel, dwelling in the ground of their own family, the joyful ones are to be relied upon by the yoginīs.
In fields, sacred places and charnel grounds, whether peopled or unpeopled, they should never be forsaken.

Women with moonlike faces and eyes like the blue lotus, anointed with the finest fragrance:
whoever, as a cause of merit, offers these with threefold purity to the master as a vajra offering,
whatever merit comes from giving land, elephants, horses, many chariots, and from bestowing daughters,
all that merit arises from this, O lord of men, at the junction of the moon and sun that dwell in the sky.

Tārā becomes on earth a low-caste woman of four kinds; Pāṇḍaravāsinī is a kṣatriya woman.
Earth is the vaiśya women, of three kinds; Māmakī, born in the family of the twice-born, becomes of seven kinds.
She who is called Sound is a bell-metal smith; Taste-vajrā is certainly a liquor seller; Form-vajrā
truly and certainly becomes a goldsmith; O lord of men, upon the earth Smell-vajrā

is renowned as a garland maker; by the power of her nature and qualities, Touch-vajrā is a weaver.
Dharmadhātuvajrā is renowned in the world as becoming a jeweler.
Cāmuṇḍī becomes a meat seller; by the power of her nature and qualities, Vaiṣṇavī is a potter.
Vārāhī certainly becomes a porter; Ṣaṇmukhī is a courtesan; Aindrī is a seamstress.

Brahmāṇī becomes a fisherwoman; and in the places of the earth, Māheśvarī becomes a dancer.
Lakṣmī, whose face is the full moon, the eighth, becomes a dyer: these are the birthplaces of the bhūtas.
Jambhanī becomes a lac worker; O lord of men, Stambhanī is a cotton worker.
She who is called Mānī is an oil presser; the very great Atibalā is a blacksmith, the fourth.

Marīcī becomes a leather worker; Bhṛkuṭī likewise is a carpenter.
Glorious Cundā is a barber; dwelling in earthly existence, Śṛṅkhalā is a bamboo worker.
Raudrākṣī becomes a well digger; the tenth, Atinīlā, is a bamboo-pole dancer.

The ten women born of the wrathful ones, portions of the wrathful ones, are to be worshipped by the yogin.

Glorious Dog-Face becomes a barbarian woman (mlecchī); O lord of men, Pig-Face is a sweeper woman;
Jackal-Face is the caṇḍālīs; and in the abode on the face of the earth, Tiger-Face is a Tājika woman;
Crow-Face is a trickster woman; very clearly and certainly, Vulture-Face is a corpse-burner woman;
Garuḍa-Face becomes glorious Bhillī; and the eighth, the Owl-Faced, are the mountain women (śabarīs).

By the division of thirty-six families, in the abodes on the earth, are the families of the yoginīs.
Dwelling in the fields, the seats, the secondary seats, the lands, the supreme villages and the glorious forests,
they bind the deluded, and on the supreme earth they grant accomplishments to the yogins.
They are four and six, likewise eight, with ten, and eight more, and the one alone, in that order.

The four are the divisions of the buddhas; then the six are the divisions of the bodhisattvas;
the wrathful ones are the divisions of the directions; in the abodes on earth, the divisions of the pretas are likewise eight;
the asuras also are divided into eight, and the yogin should know the nāgas who dwell in existence.
The one alone is the sovereign of the manifold, the pervader of the glorious families in the abode of the three worlds.

In the underworlds are the eight caṇḍālīs; in the human world, in the maṇḍala of the ten directions, dwell those born of wrath;
the so-called pretas are in the world of the pretas; in the supreme abode of the gods are the six beginning with Śabdavajrā;
in the realm of Brahmā are the four glorious ones; in the supreme city of peace is the one Mother of the three realms.
Those with fiercely wrathful faces draw in the manifold; the pleased ones are the protectors.

Girls, old women and young women, those whose bodies are stained, brahmin women and kṣatriya women,
vaiśya women, śūdra women and outcastes, or those lacking eye or hand, with ears, lips or nose cut off:
all these are to be worshipped as a cause of awakening, through wisdom and means, O king, by the master
whose mind is endowed with compassion, who is free of stains, and who follows the conduct of awakening.

First, the woman becomes the secret mudrā; in the samaya, the glorious supreme man is the mudrā;
the limbs of play become the karmamudrā; the equal bliss of the two organs is the dharmamudrā.
The five scents of the messengers (dūtīs), dwelling in body and lotus, are their five families:
by the force of their natural qualities, they are the scents of musk, of lotus, of urine, of flesh, and of putrefaction.

Glorious Bhadrā, and certainly Padminī, the Water-Goer, Citriṇī and Hastinī
are called glorious Tārā and the White-Clad One (Pāṇḍarā), and, according to family, Māmakī and Locanā as well.
The yogin becomes lion, deer, horse, bull and elephant; according to the divisions of family,
these are Akṣobhya, Amoghasiddhi, Him of the Stainless Jewel in Hand, Padmapāṇi and Cakrin.

With slender limbs and fine hair, soft hands and feet, pleasing and gentle, is glorious Subhadrā.
Somewhat slender and tall, with unmoving eyes and curling hair, is Padminī.
Shameless, of fierce desire, delighting in much quarreling, with very short hair, is Śaṅkhinī.
Tall, with all her limbs complete, her secret region certainly light, and with long hair, is Citriṇī.

Stout and short, with firm limbs, her secret region very hard, and with thick hair, is Hastinī.
Here the pure families of the messengers occur in some cases; at all times the families are mixed.
Lions dwell in the wilds, without a homeland, fearless, of generous disposition.
Deer are swift of gait, light in their secret region, of frightened mind, and very fearful.

The horse certainly craves desire, is under others' sway, has the scent of urine, and seeks the aims of others.
The bull has stiff eyes and a slow gait; by natural quality he is dull, and has the scent of fish.
The elephant is lustful, certainly slow in walking, with the scent of putrefaction, and greatly deluded.
Divided by the thirty-six divisions, in the abodes on the earth, is the nature of the scents of the families.

For the sake of worship, the yogin should know the treatise on desire, the abode of many qualities.
Even if great joy is made, if the yoginī is not satisfied with the yogin, she will not grant accomplishment.
They are the Beautiful One, the goddess, the piśācī, the human woman, the rākṣasī and the nāgī.
The Beautiful One becomes glorious Dharmadhātu; by force of her quality, the goddess is Śabdavajrā.

The piśācī becomes Gandhavajrā; the human women are Rūpavajrā, O lord of men;
whoever is the vicious rākṣasī, she is certainly Rasavajrā; the nāgī is Sparśavajrā.
The Beautiful One benefits beings, delights in definite vows, and is of a disposition of restraint and meditative concentration;
the goddess is attached to enjoyments; the piśācī is stained and attached to leftovers.

The human woman is attached to desire; the rākṣasī delights in human blood and has a murderous mind;
the nāgīs have milk as their food. On the supreme earth they are to be worshipped by the yogin.
Likewise elsewhere, the yogin should know their natures by the force of their natural qualities.
In the abodes on the earth, the sky-goers and earth-goers are divided into thirty-six divisions.

Liquor is of the nature of wisdom: with honey, born of molasses, born of rice, or born of trees.
Whoever drinks it without a mudrā becomes a slave to sense objects, surrounded by the hosts of Māra.

Therefore, endowed with surpassing wisdom, it steals away the stain of sin and grants accomplishments to the mantrins.
Here, for the sake of the drinking, any mudrā whatsoever should be taken, even one devoid of samaya.

If the king, the hare-marked one alone, relied on at all times, steals away the fear of death,
what need is there to speak of yogins who abide in the source of phenomena of wisdom together with the sun?
Akṣobhya and Amoghasiddhi, together with the supreme Jina, are endowed with the dog, the horse, the cow and the elephant.
The vajra-staff of the afflictions, the folk of the beasts, the eighth, the giver of fear: these others are exceedingly fierce.

Dog, horse, cow, elephant, sheep, goat, deer, donkey, pig and camel: these are the directions.
Kumbhīra and the leech, kulīra, fish and makara, frog, tortoise and conch;
rhinoceros, tiger and bear, together with mongoose, the female yak, jackal, otter and cat,
the wild dog together with the lion: the eight and the ten, these are the bhūta-born and the wrath-born.

Godhā, rat, śālijāta, monkey and hare, the dhāraṇī creature, iṣu and the lizard: eight.
The proud bird and the parrot, the cuckoos and the partridge, clearly the ocean.
Lāva, pigeon, the other called the white bird, caṭaka, the ruddy goose and the swan,
the glorious crane, the cuckoo-eye, rājakī and bhagavatī, tittiri and the laughing goose,

the water-diver and the heron, together with the rest: these eights are to be known in order.
Crow, vulture, owl, the enemy of deer, the crested one, the hen and bhetrāghāra,
the horpa and the other, the enemy of trees: these ten are born of wrath and belong to the family of the wrathful ones.
The blue-eye, the glorious cakora, anīla, gudmu, khabuk-ki, the one that sleeps upon its feet,

bheruṇḍa and ambarīka, the eighth, are the supreme birds, O lord of men!
With this division into thirty-six families, abiding in earthly existence, those that move on the ground and those that move in the sky:
all the samaya substances belonging to these families the yogin should eat at the time of worship.
If, deluded, out of bewilderment he rejects them, O lord of men, he will swiftly fall into degeneration.

Teeth, hair, skin and the rest, with flesh, with sinews, bones, heart and lotus,
lice and body hair, worms, and, most excellent, O lord of men, lungs, intestines, bladder,
phlegm, bile, water and pus, and the various things that dwell in the body: blood, sweat and fat,
tears, saliva, mucus, urine, lymph, grease, color, smell and feces,

tongue, eye, ear and nose, together with the hare-marked one and the suns: with these one should worship the deities.
The thirty-six syllables, by the power of their nature and qualities, and the factors of awakening as well,
all the divisions of the thirty-six elements, the families dwelling in the body, the emblems and the mudrās,
the thirty-six yoga tantras abiding on the grounds: here, for the yoginīs,

water, arghya, scent, incense, flowers and unspoiled fruit, lamps,
divine food offerings, and here they become garments: these ten, also in the gatherings of the circle.
Semen, urine, marrow and feces, flesh, liver, bile and blood,
intestines and skin: these, O king, become the ten kinds, also in the gatherings of the circle.

The vajra, the sword, the arrow, the hundred-petaled lotus, and fifth, the emblem of the wheel;
the vīṇā and the mirror become the vessel, O lord of men, and garlands of flowers and garment;
sixth, the source of phenomena, is certainly in the palm of the hand: these are the emblems of Śabdavajrā and the others.
Likewise the curved knife and the rest, the one called the vase, and likewise the ladle, the yellow garment,

the needle or else the hammer, very clear and certain, the fishing net and the trident;
the eighth emblem of the glorious goddesses becomes the stone, O lord of men: these belong to the bhūta-born.
Of the Stupefier and the others: the red vessel like the sun, the silkworm, kuśa grass,
the water knife and sandals, the one called the razor, likewise wooden sandals, and the parasol,

the crest ornament and the bamboo staff: these become the ten own emblems of the wrath-born.
The cow horn and the bowl, the strung bow, and the meat skewer that is in the palm of the hand,
the subsidiary limb of the vīṇā, the arrow and the peacock's tail: here these become eight.
These most excellent divisions of thirty-six emblems the yogin should worship upon the ground.

Again, the śūdra caste and the others become emblems by the power of their qualities: the utpala or the plowshare.

For the kṣatriya woman it becomes a jeweled silk pendant; O lord of men, the pen and the jewel garland
Are likewise for the vaiśya woman; and likewise, together with the water-dweller, the copper vessel is for the brahmin woman.
The signs of the consort are of four kinds: the ḍamaru, the great drum, the crest ornament, and the rosary.

Feces and urine, together with blood and flesh: the goddesses become these four.
Ear, nose, eye, tongue, anus and bhaga: Śabdavajrā and the others, six.
Pus, phlegm, lice, worms, lymph, fat, body hair and head hair: these eight as well.
Intestines, bile, bone and marrow, abiding variously in the body, liver and lungs,
Channels and skin, heart and suet: these become ten, O lord of men.
Ear and nose, eye and mouth, anus, middle, bhaga, armpit and so on:
The impurities that abide in the eight limbs of the body, O lord of men, become the eight asura women.
With the nails, the teeth and the twelve limbs, the skull yoginīs become eight groups of eight.

Feces, urine, blood and flesh, abiding variously in the body, are four in the divisions of the pīṭhas.
Ear, nose, eye, tongue, anus and bhaga are six in the divisions of the kṣetras.
Pus and the rest up to head hair, in the places on the earth, are the eight divisions of the chandohas.
Intestines and the rest up to suet, which are called the directions, O lord of men, are the divisions of the Samāja.
Ear and the rest, in the body of eight limbs, the many kinds of impurity, are the divisions of the charnel grounds.
Kālāgni, the moon, the sun and Rāhu, clear and certain, are four in the divisions of the pīṭhas.
Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn and the nāgas are six in the divisions of the kṣetras.
Earth, water, fire and wind, and the earth-born, water-born, fire-born and wind-born:
These eight are the divisions of the chandohas. And again, likewise, the six tastes, smell and color,
Touch and sound: these clearly are, in just that way, the ten divisions of the Samāja.
The totalities of earth, water, fire and wind are in front, on the left, on the right and behind;
The four, color and the rest, dissolve into the intermediate directions: these are the divisions of the eight charnel grounds.

The pīṭhas are the houses of Tārā and the others; clearly, Śabdavajrā and the others are likewise called the kṣetras.
The chandohas become Carcikā and the others; the Samāja, O lord of men, is Stambhanī and the others.
The charnel grounds are Śvānāsyā and the others, abiding at the supreme ground; together with the root place and the secret place,
These are the two houses of the consort; clearly, on the earth, they are the outcaste woman and the outcaste woman.
The pīṭha becomes the secret lotus of the woman; in the pledges, the vajra itself is the upapīṭha.
Kṣetra and chandoha, the Samāja and the charnel ground on the earth: likewise, thus are they all.
The pīṭha is the left limbs and the front; the others, and likewise the right, are upapīṭhas.
Thus all of them, the kṣetras and the rest, abide in the hands and feet, at the boundaries of the fingertips and toe-tips.

The applications of mindfulness, too, become the goddesses counted as the oceans, that is, four; this is renowned in Kālacakra.
Wisdom, a factor of awakening, is the consort, and the others are Śabdavajrā and the rest, six.
The ocean of right abandonments and the other ocean, the bases of miraculous power, become eight.
The five wrathful ones are the powers, clearly; and the faculties are certainly five.
The noble eightfold path, O lord of men, becomes the eight born of the asuras.
By this division into thirty-seven, in the abode of the three realms of existence, are the factors of awakening;
All of these the yogi should know as the yoginīs in the places upon the earth.
Thus all the pīṭhas and the rest, O lord of men, are likewise outer and also within the body.

The Buddhist, the Śaiva, or the naked ascetic, the one called the Fortunate, and likewise the brahmin of pure abode,
The skull-bearer (kāpālika), the hair-plucker, the white-clad, the one of the kṣetrapāla lineage, and the sage,
Those who take the form of madmen, those whose minds are free of evil, the learned, and the renowned: these he becomes.
The yogi, for the sake of accomplishment, attains that very treasure of all qualities, O lord of men.

The thumb and the index finger, and likewise the middle finger and the ring finger,
And at the end of them, certainly, the little finger: the yogi should know them as treasures of all qualities.
For the sake of the mudrās they become distinctions of names, by virtue of their qualities, in the order of the fingers.
When binding is spoken of, it becomes the vajra binding, and so too, certainly, the binding of the fist.
A fist upon the thigh, seated in the vajra posture: this becomes the vajra mudrā of the Lord of Jinas.
In the cross-legged posture the left hand rests on the lap, and the right reaches the ground from the side of the knee:
The earth-touching is the mudrā of Akṣobhya; again, the right hand granting boons is that of Ratnapāṇi.
The right hand resting upon the left, palm upward, abiding in equipoise, becomes that of Padmapāṇi.
The left on the cross-legged lap, the palm of the other hand fearless: that is of Khaḍgapāṇi.
The right fist upon the not-right, and certainly, again, the index finger of the other is bound within the fist,
Clearly reaching the heart: this is the mudrā of Vairocana. The other, the mudrā of the wheel of Dharma,
Has index finger and thumb joined like a bracelet, with the middle finger and the rest extended.

In the left hand the full and stainless hare-bearer (the moon), in the right the vajra-sun;
Abiding in the union of sun and moon, the terrifying one born of the wrathful vajra, the five-pronged:
Having meditated on it, one should touch the limbs with it, together with the crest, beginning from the head and down to the feet.
This, O king, is the supreme and glorious mudrā of Kālacakra, which removes the stain of evil.

Whatever things are to be taken, however slight, born of earth, born of water, born of a womb, born of warmth and moisture, and so on,
food and drink, with diligence, and the guru, and his feet as well, are to be touched with the mudrā.
Whatever action is to be undertaken, one should apply to it the mudrā that fits it by virtue of its quality.
On the ground and so on for the sake of the maṇḍala, and also when walking on foot, the yogin should strike it.

With the hands bound in the vajra bond, it surely becomes the mudrā of the great lord of the wrathful ones.
With the first and last fingers bound and the fist bound, it is that of the chief, Trailokyavijaya.
The remaining forms of the signs are clearly and surely those of the gods and goddesses.
With the hands bound in the vajra bond, in the form of a swaying hood, it becomes the mudrā of the nāgas.

The thumbs and little fingers joined, like the petals of a lotus, the middle fingers extended,
the two index fingers as a half-moon, the ring fingers bent below, reaching one's own palms:
this mudrā becomes the five-pronged vajra of the vajra-holder, and so it is to be shown.
The fingers in the form of spokes, thumbs and all the rest, in the palms of both hands.

The two hands bound in the vajra bond become the vajra; with vajra fists, together with the bell.
A half fist, with index and middle fingers like an arrow, becomes the sharp sword.
For the trident, the index finger and the others, three points, spread out again with a half fist.
The glorious curved knife: the fist bound and the glorious little finger as a half-moon, it becomes terrifying.

The fist bound above the ear and the thumb upon the middle finger: this is for the excellent arrow.
The fist bound and the index finger sharply crooked, O lord of men, is likewise for the hook.
The index finger at the base of the ring finger, and the middle finger above, like an arrow, is for the spear;
the fist held straight is for the staff; the palm perfectly level with the thumb extended is for the axe.

Two fists above with the index fingers are for the elephant hide of the lord of the asuras.
For the fang, in the palms of both hands the fist is bound and the little finger is a half-moon.
For the shield (kheṭaka), the left arm is fully extended and the palm turned upward.
For the khaṭvāṅga staff, the fist is made hollow and the little finger surely extended to the shoulder.

The hand like a lotus petal, its fingers without gaps, turned upward, is for the glorious skull cup.
For the bow, the hand lies palm up as a fist, and the left arm is surely fully extended.
The middle fingers wholly supported by the crooked index fingers are surely for the vajra noose.
For the jewel, the fingers are joined, O lord of men; those same fingers spread apart are for the lotus.

For the water creature, index finger and thumb are joined, with the fist below the thumb.
For the mirror, the perfectly level palm with fingers without gaps is turned to face forward.
For the chain, the index finger and the others above the thumb are crooked and arranged in sequence.
For the head, the four fingers beginning with the thumb are shaped like a face, and the little finger is bent below.

For the diadem, the hands make the conch mudrā, with the two index fingers joined.
The five fingers joined as one on the backs of the palms are for the earrings.
The fingers arranged in sequence and bound on the backs of the hands are for the necklace.
Three fingers of both hands joined together, the first and last bent, are for the girdle.

With the fist bound, the thumbs and the two middle fingers made into a circle are for the anklets.
Likewise, for the pair of armlets and for the bracelets, the index fingers are joined together.
The thumb becomes the family of the supreme ḍākinīs; the index finger, that of the guhyakas;
of the gandharvas and the nāgas, the middle and ring fingers, in sequence.

Of the bhūtas, the glorious little finger; the most excellent palm becomes the family of the rākṣasas.
Of the siddhas, the bound fist, the supreme family; the joints of the fingers belong to the gods.
In the palms of both hands, the five fingers half crooked are the family mudrā of the clawed beasts.
From the bound fist, the two index fingers half crooked and surely reaching the head are of the horned beasts.

On uneven palms, the little fingers bound together, the wings joined, are of the egg-born.
The five fingers with their tips crooked become the distinguished family mudrā of the nāgas.
For the torma of each day and night, the tips of the index fingers are fully extended and the others joined below.
For the blazing ones, the thumbs are joined; another form has the index fingers of the supreme palms extended.

Showing the index finger surely says, "Have you come well?" and this is also said to the yogin.
With two, "Most welcome," and with the thumb bound, "Fortunate one, virtuous one," is expressed.
Snapping the fingers surely says, "You here are a supreme mantra practitioner."
With the thumb and the ring finger: "You are satisfied with wine endowed with the pledge."

Extending the tips of all the fingers says, "Fortunate one, welcome," and this is also said to the yogin.
Touching a limb on the left makes it clear: "You are ever my one and only kinsman."
By touching the vulva, O lord, the lips and the two breasts, and surely by pressing with the fingernails,
and by interlocking the fingers and extending the middle finger and the thumb, the pledge is spoken.

If the lips, brows and eyes move and one shakes the head, it expresses utter bewilderment.
The little finger between the fangs and the index finger at the heart and mouth clearly show fear.
With the thumb in a bound fist, and striking the ground with hands and feet: "I shall eat."
Touching the tongue: "I have eaten"; striking the belly and the teeth: "I have not eaten."

With the back of the hand one surely says, "Go!"; with the palm turned toward one, "Sit, sit!"

Rubbing the shin against the thigh definitely says, "Fortunate one, you shall rest this very day."
Stretching out the feet means sleep; stretching out both knees means, "Give me great joy."
Touching all the limbs and placing the hand at the mouth means, "For me there is no gathering."

Clasping each other's hands says, "Today in my house there is a gathering of the circle."
With the tips of the thumb and the ring finger: "Worship as you wish with the many kinds of pledge substances."
Shaking the foot means, likewise, going to a gathering outside.
Interlacing the fingers with one another means, "Vajra friend, today you take away fear."

Biting off one's hair with one's own teeth says, "You beast of a man, here you shall fall."
Making the teeth chatter against each other means, "Today I shall eat this flesh of yours."
Moving the tongue and lips definitely says, "The blood of your body shall be drunk."
Setting the teeth upon the lip means, "I shall eat your entrails that lie within your belly."

By the gesture of charm it becomes charm, O lord of men; by the gesture of laughter, laughter.
By the gesture of dance it becomes dance; by the gesture of many kinds of music, music.
By the gesture of song, song; by the gestures of scent, of many supreme qualities, scent.
By the gesture of a garland it becomes a garland; by the power of qualities, by the gestures of incense, incense.

In the form of a lamp it is a lamp that surely destroys darkness, and the mudrā of the vessel becomes nectar.
In this way all the mudrās are further divided by a fivefold division,
and the other, endless mudrās, all of which abide in the body, the yogin should know.
Whatever becomes the nature of things upon the earth, their natures too are mudrās.

By a sidelong glance the female messenger says, "Fortunate one, where have you come from?"
Placing his hand on his head and looking directly toward her, the yogin speaks his reply.
By looking upward: "Are you well?" By looking upon the ground: "Sit and rest."
By looking to the left: "Go." By a passionate look the messenger expresses great joy.

By a peaceful look: "My friend." By a frowning, wrathful look, fear is plainly shown.
By looking at the hair she declares, "I am savage"; to the fortunate one she shows her own nature with a glance from the corner of the eye.
By looking at the brow-tuft: "I am the supreme yoginī." By looking at the nose she makes her qualities plain.
By looking at the lips: "Fortunate one." When she looks at her two breasts she declares, "I am an excellent mudrā."

By looking at the heart: "I am one who has meditated." When she looks at her two hands she declares, "I am exceedingly fierce."
By looking between the shoulders: "I am a śakti." When she looks at the palm of the hand together with the nails: "a rākṣasī."
When she looks at her back: "a hand-goer." By looking at the navel: "O lord of men, I am one who keeps the pledges."
By looking at the secret place: "I am pure." By looking at the thigh: "I am greatly joyful and hard to tame."

By looking at the knees she definitely declares, "I am accomplished." By looking at the feet: "a giver of magical powers."
When she looks directly at the big toes: "In existence I am vajra-bodied, the sole heroine."
By looking at the tips of all the toes: "Going everywhere in the abode of the three existences, Viśvamātā."
These very ways of looking of the female messengers, in places upon the earth, the yogin should know.

Of the body, a sixth part: the hands, the feet, and even the head, the supreme limb of the body, are to be given;
among the organs of action, speech, and among the senses, the mind together with its qualities;
among the elements, a portion of the elements; of bipeds and herds of cattle, a portion of those very things, and others besides.
When these are offered to the master who knows the supreme mudrā, one goes to the abode of bliss.

Those sentient beings who dwell in the threefold existence, from the world realms, with the vajra hook of wisdom,
are summoned, all of them, with supreme compassion, into the maṇḍala, and with the nectar of the buddhas and of the vajra
they are empowered; then, having gained the path of the vajra-holder, with a body like the stainless hare-marked one (the moon),
free of stains and abiding in the conduct of awakening, they are to be sent back to their own places.

The empowerment without substances, by command of the lord of Jinas, is to be given to one who has renounced everything;
likewise to one endowed with the yoga of the channels, a treasure of the paths beginning with the cow and of all qualities,
for the sake of the teaching: the hand mudrā, the empowerment that has reached one's own heart, freed from all faults.
To other, inferior ones, the worship of the Mother, the heart essence of the supreme Victorious One, is not to be given at all.

At the end of the empowerment, the soft upper cloths and pairs of garments of the glorious vases, pleasing to the body,
are to be given to the glorious yoginīs, and likewise other upper cloths and pairs of garments besides.
To those who stand at the gates, and to all the virtuous kinds, give generously according to one's ability, and likewise others.
At the end, having performed the fire offering, one should settle the wisdom being in the lotus of one's own heart.

The worldly ones are to be escorted to their own places, and all the colored powders likewise to a pure stream.
Betel, scent, incense, and flowers with fruits, and bolts of cloth are given to the young maidens;
then the master together with the disciples worships the whole assembly as desired,
grants leave to the disciples, and requests the most excellent assembly to depart, each to its own place.

Fear of enemies, lions, lordly elephants, fire, the lord of mahoragas, thieves, binding with nooses,
the raging of the Lord of the Waters, piśācas, the terrors of death, disease, harm from Indra,
poverty, separation from women, fear of turmoil from kings, vajra transgressions, and loss of one's aims:
whoever remembers the feet of the yoginīs day and night, for him all these are utterly destroyed.

This is the third chapter, on empowerment, from glorious Kālacakra, extracted from the great glorious Ādibuddha.

"O Begetter of the Victors, I have received the seven empowerments, and the vase and secret empowerments,
the wisdom-gnosis empowerment that destroys the fears of existence, and the fourth, which yogis must traverse.
Now I request once more the true means of accomplishment of the Universal Lord together with the supreme Victor."
Having heard the words of Sucandra, the Lord of Victors taught the means of accomplishment for the vajra-holders as well.

One body, like the moon; a pair of feet; three necks; four faces, glorious and of various colors;
six shoulders, twelve upper arms, twenty-four lotus hands, and three hundred sixty joints;
his feet upon the disks of moon, sun, and fire, Māra and Rudra in terror,
trampled in play; equal in existence and non-existence: that sole Kālacakra is to be accomplished.

In a pleasure grove, on a mountain, at a place of the supreme Victor, in an empty temple of a god,
in a place of siddhas, a charnel ground, by a lake, at an auspicious place, and likewise in a hidden spot:
wherever the mind becomes fully content, there one should practice, O lord of men!
Seated on soft bedding and a cushion, having first performed the protection described before,

one should first visualize, at the heart in the center of a moon, that very one, sending various rays into the ten directions.
Having purified the mouth and so forth, then, to the Victors emanated into the sky,
one makes offerings of various kinds and confesses the many kinds of wrongdoing one has amassed;
then, with body, speech, and mind made pure, the practitioner should go for refuge to the Three.

"Whatever pure virtue the perfect buddhas, the bodhisattvas, and the assembly of noble ones have done,
in all of it I rejoice, abiding in the conduct of awakening, freed from wrongdoing.
In the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha, who take away the fears of existence, I take refuge until awakening.
'May I become a perfect buddha': this aspiration I make here for the sake of sentient beings."

Empty, the intrinsic nature of the essence of things: all these beings are utterly devoid of things.
Therefore there is no buddha, no awakening, no compassion that benefits others, and no commitment to signs.
Having understood all of it thus, even so, O lord of men, the vajra of body, speech, and mind,
lord of the maṇḍala of immeasurable qualities, should be meditated upon in the maṇḍala by bodhisattvas.

First, here in the middle of the body, those devoted to restraint should make fire be extinguished by water.
After that, earth turns to water and, like salt, dissolves into the midst of the water.
Wind dries up those waters and swiftly departs, becoming invisible, into space.
The mind, free of objects, should be set in the central ground, at the end of fire and darkness.

Space, wind, fire, water, and earth, the mountain of the gods, a lotus, moon, sun, and fire;
a storied palace radiating stainless rays on every side, or tents born of the vajra;
in its center, on vajra ground, a maṇḍala radiating hosts of jewel rays,
and, born from gnosis out of the syllable oṃ, the supreme Victor's lotus, with seats of moon and sun.

Outside that is the speech maṇḍala; this jewel lotus is without moon and sun.
In the outer directions and corners are lotuses of the day-maker, and in the middle of the doors, chariots.
At the twelve doors are royal archways made of jewels and gold, and charnel grounds;
in the core, sixteen pillars of vajra nature, of the Victor's wheel of complete enjoyment.

On the moon and sun, with a and the rest and ka and the rest, together with the vajra, through the five beginning with the mirror,
he emits five rays: one should meditate on Kālacakra, radiating stainless light.
His body is endowed with vajra ornaments; twenty-four lotus hands, twelve upper arms, four faces,
three necks, and twelve eyes; his faces are wide open, half-baring their fangs.

On the Lord of Victors' lotus, born of the glorious syllable oṃ, upon moon, sun, and fire,
on the hearts of the two, Rudra and the Bodiless One, the Lord of Victors, in great play, stands with right leg extended.
Beneath his supreme red right foot is Māra, white; and beneath the left is Rudra.
His central, right, and left necks are colored like a bee, like the sun, and like the moon.

His shoulders are blue, red, and white like the hare-bearer (the moon), on the right and on the left;
the pairs of upper arms on right and left are black, the hue of the sun, and the color of the moon;
likewise his hands, holding weapons, are in order three groups of eight.
On the lotus hands, upon the hare-marked one, the five fingers of three joints each radiate the five colors.

In the palms of the supreme hands, black, red, and white, a host of weapons rightly rests:
vajra and sword, trident, the curved knife that terrifies existence, and the fire arrow;
then the vajra hook, the ḍamaru resounding, the hammer, and the wheel;
the spear, the staff, and the axe: these are in the vajra-holder's twelve right lotus hands.

Bell, shield, khaṭvāṅga, a wide-mouthed skull cup filled with blood,
bow, noose, jewel, lotus, conch, mirror, and iron chain,
and the four-faced head of Brahmā, adorned with a lotus: these are in the Victor's left hands.
The goddesses of Māra and Rudra cling to the soles of his feet, their faces turned down.

Golden in color, with four faces, embracing him with eight lotus hands, is Viśvamātā.
In her right hands, in order, are curved knife, hook, ḍamaru resounding, and rosary;
in her left, skull cup, noose, hundred-petaled lotus, and likewise the supreme jewel.
Her left leg extended, with twelve eyes, she is sealed with the mudrās, with the Lord of Victors as her crown ornament.

On the eight petals are the eight goddesses, with eight lotus hands, the number of the Vasus, four faces, the number of the Vedas, and twelve eyes, the number of the suns.
Of these, four hold serpent fly-whisks; they are in the corners, distinguished by the faces of the Jinas.
In the corners of Īśāna, Nairṛti, Agni the Crested One, and Vāyu are the Dharma conch and the gaṇḍī gong,
and likewise it becomes the wish-fulfilling jewel, and in the same way, in order, the wish-granting tree.

The Dark Goddess holds in the palm of her first hand a vessel of incense, in her second a vessel of cooling scent,
in her third a red compound, and in her fourth right hand the hare-bearer, that is, camphor, together with musk;
in her left hands, in order, a bell, a lotus, a flower of the divine tree, and a garland of flowers.
In the right hands of the Red Goddess are a lamp, necklaces, a crown, and bracelets; in her left hands

are a garment, a girdle, earrings radiating stainless light, and anklets.
In the right hands of the Yellow Goddess are, in order, a conch, a flute, a jewel, and a ḍamaru drum;
in her left, a vīṇā, a drum, staffs that ring loudly with jingling, and a copper horn.
Vessels of milk, water, medicine, liquor, nectar, savors, fruit, and food belong to the White Goddess.

On the lotuses of the directions are the buddhas, four, the number of the oceans, each with nine eyes, three faces, the number of the fires, and six arms, the number of the seasons.
In the corners are Tārā and the other goddesses, and again, at their eight junctions, eight vases.
The gods and goddesses are black, red, yellow, and white like the hare-bearer, the moon;
the black and the white are upon moons, or else are those in the intermediate directions; the red and the yellow are upon suns.

Ākāśagarbha and the others stand on the walls, in the directions and intermediate directions, together with Sparśavajrā and the others.
On the right and left lotuses of the east and the other directions are Samantabhadra and likewise
glorious Vajrapāṇi, and indeed Śabdavajrā and Dharmadhātu, in order.
Jambhaka, Stambhaka, and Mānaka, and the one called Atibala: that gatekeeper is in the east.

Wisdom is in the lap; method is on a lotus with the hare-bearer, the moon; the goddesses belong to the gods.
The two embrace each other with their lotus hands, each marked by its own emblem.
Whatever emblem is in the palm of someone's first right hand, that is his seal, and the lotus is the lesser.
With wisdom and method abiding in the bliss of the supreme wheel, they sit in the lotus and vajra postures.

In the right hands of the black ones are a sword, a curved knife, and the trident that terrifies existence;
in the palms of the left hands, a shield, a skull cup, and a khaṭvāṅga, which is white.
An arrow, a vajra hook, and a resounding ḍamaru are in the right hands, in order;
a bow, a noose, and a stainless radiant jewel are likewise in the left hands of the red ones.

In the right hands of the yellow ones are, in order, a wheel, a staff, and a fearsome vajra;
in the left, a glorious conch, a chain, and indeed a resounding vajra bell.
In the right hands of those white like the hare-bearer are a glorious hammer, a spear, and a trident;
in the left, a white lotus with a hundred petals, a mirror, and a rosary.

In the right hands of the green ones are, in order, a vajra, a curved knife, and an axe;
in the left, a bell, a skull cup, treasury of all qualities, and the very head of Brahmā.
For the blue goddesses too, it should be understood likewise, according to their nature and qualities.
Black, red, white, and like supreme gold are the goddesses Earth of the east and the others.

Leaving aside the eight goddesses Dhūmā and the rest, sometimes, on the lotuses of the Lord of Jinas,
in the center of the heart of the glorious wheel, it also becomes the embodiment of the twenty-five, the lord of persons.
Knowing the power of their own minds, yogins should meditate on this Blessed One too.
For the sake of empowerment, in maṇḍalas according to the family, the outer wheel becomes greatly reduced.

Outside, on the eight lotus petals, with the eight times eight, are the goddesses of the eight directions,
Carcikā and the other yoginīs, without moon and sun, with four arms, the number of the Vedas, and three eyes.
On the eastern lotus is Carcikā; in Agni's quarter, she who rides the lord of birds; Vārāhī and the Six-Faced Goddess
in the quarters of Yama and Nairṛti; with Varuṇa and in Vāyu's quarter, the Vajra-Handed Goddess and the Four-Faced Goddess.

Raudrī and Lakṣmī are in the north and in Īśāna's quarter; holding weapons, they embrace the bodies of their method partners.
The yoginīs of the eight times eight who dwell on the lotus petals have the colors and faces of their chief goddess.
In the east and so on, in the palms of the first hands, are a curved knife, a trident, a wheel, a club,
a staff, a sword, a short spear, and a hook, on the pair of right lotus hands;

a vajra, an arrow, a lotus, a stainless Brahmā staff like lightning, a trident,
a resounding ḍamaru bound with various jewels, a lotus, and a rosary.
In the left are a skull cup, a khaṭvāṅga, a lotus, a conch, a chain, a shield,
indeed a jewel, and a noose; and a vajra bell that loudly resounds, a bow,

a water pot, a khaṭvāṅga, a snake, then a water-born lotus, and a jewel.
Such are the weapons in the hands of the eight times eight yoginīs on the eight-petaled lotuses.
The Terrifying One, the Fierce One, Fang of Time, the Blazing-Faced, She of the Wind's Speed, the Very Violent,
the Fierce-Eyed, and the Great-Nosed are in the directions on Carcikā's eight-petaled lotus.

Fortune, Illusion, Fame, Supreme Glory, the Fully Victorious, the Glorious Victorious One, the Glorious Victory-Maker,
and eighth, the Glorious Wheel-Bearer, are on the eight-petaled lotus in the quarter of Vaiṣṇavī.
The Skeleton, Night of Time, the Furious-Faced, Tongue of Time, the Grimacing One,
the Black, the Dreadful, and the Ugly-Formed are on the eight-petaled lotus of the goddess of Vārāhī's petal.

Lotus, the Bodiless, the Maiden, She Who Rides the Lord of Beasts, Jewel Garland, the Fair-Eyed,
the Putrid, and the Good are on the lotus petals where the chief is the supreme peacock-rider, O king!
Vajra Light, Vajra Body, the Supreme Golden One, Urvaśī, Citralekhā,
Rambhā, the Sorrowless, and Supreme Tārā are on the eight-petaled lotus of the Vajra-Handed Goddess, who has the presiding deity.

Sāvitrī, Lotus-Eyed, and surely the Water-Born One, Intelligence, and the Lady of Speech,
Gāyatrī, Lightning, and Memory too are on the lotus of the one whose presiding deity is the Veda-faced.
Gaurī, Gaṅgā, the Eternal One, the Exceedingly Supremely Swift One, Totalā, and the Indicating One,
the Tawny One and the Black One: these eight likewise are on the jewel-lotus petals where Raudrī is chief.

The Glorious White One, Moon-Streak, Hare-Bearer-Faced, the Swan-Colored One, and the Holder,
the Lotus Mistress, Star-Eye, the Stainless One, and the Hare-Bearing One are with their signs on the lotuses of Īśāna.
Outside that, on the sun lotuses, are the asura, the Mover, Yama, Agni, and Ṣaṇmukha (Kārttikeya),
the yakṣa, Śakra (Indra), the Lord of the Waters, the Faced One, the Lord of Creatures (Paśupati), the Ocean, the glorious Lord of the Gaṇas, and Viṣṇu.

Sword, curved knife, tree, the flower of the tree of the lord of the gods, staff, sword, and spear,
staff and spear, lance, jewel, club, vajra, and fire-arrow,
needle and rosary are in the palms of the hands; trident, arrow, and noose,
jewel, axe, vajra, wheel, and staff come to be in the right hands of the Haras.

On the left are shield, skull cup, a jewel that is not white, utpala, and iron chain,
noose, water-born lotus, and water vase: these come in order to be in the left hands, O lord of men.
Jewel and mirror, and likewise with a mongoose, the water-born, the vajra bell, and the bow,
the lotus, the water vase, the snake, the bow, and likewise the nāga noose, and the jewel,

the jewel noose, and the lotus, and the conch Pāñcajanya belong to the Enemy of the Asuras.
On the lotus petals are the lunar days of Caitra and the other months; on the hub, the two complete ones.
All the supreme digits of the moon, at the time of emptiness in the worlds, should be known through the reckoning of the year.
At the doors the goddesses stand on chariots, holding sword and vajra, with hook and with arrow in hand;

glorious, with wheel and staff in hand, and by the power of the qualities of nature, with hammer and lance in hand;
glorious, with curved knife and vajra in hand, and surely an axe in hand, a trident in hand: these are on the right.
In the left hands are shield and snake, and by the power of the qualities of nature, noose and bow in hand;
glorious, with conch and jewel in hand, and likewise lotus, hare-bearer, and mirror in hand.

Glorious, with bell and skull cup in hand, and surely with a hand-goer in hand, and the khaṭvāṅga in hand.
Marīcī and the others are single-faced; the lotuses of the hands of time should be known in order.
